I was curious about this video about how América pre-Columbus formed a new empire with the Spaniards. Now I'm sure that anglos had the things very wrong in this story, Spaniards didn´t conquer América in truth the same native population conquered América with the help of the Spaniards.





By the way Mexicas is the real name of Aztecs and the real conqueror of Tenochtitlan is a woman named Malinche, She suffer a lot in the hands of the mexicas,  released by Cortez, she helped Cortez in organizing all the enemy tribes of the Mexicas and guide Cortes to conquer Tenochtitlan, she was really important in diplomacy and intelligence to help this new coalition against the  cannibal empire.





The daughters of Moctezuma all 3 married Spaniards.

The tlaxcaltecas were the first in joining the anti -mexica coalition and the first to have a legal document agreeing joining the crown as a kingdom under the laws, obligations and protection of the empire. Later their soldiers join the empire army in the conquest of south-america and Philippines, Also the tlaxcaltecas fight in Europe in many wars.





Atahualpa was seen as a traitor in the eyes of the Inca empire, he murdered his brother Huscar, also he destroyed the empire in a civil war with the help of rebel generals like Rumiñahui. Pizzaro came to current Ecuador with an army of several thousand natives and 200 Spaniards. At this time a man helped Pizarro with the language, diplomacy and intelligence. He told Pizarro about the customs of the empire, the death of Huscar and Atahualpa's war.  





This man named Felipillo told Pizarro to capture Atahualpa in order to obtain a ransom. Felipillo´s native village was destroyed in the war thanks to Atahualpa so when the Inca was captured he deceived Pizzaro telling him that an army was coming to liberate Atahualpa but in fact no army and no ransom  was coming to Pizarro location. In this manner Felipillo consumed his vengeance.





When Pizarro's army came to cusco he was surprised that  everyone received him with cheers and happiness, afterwards he founded the city of Lima.





Some years later Pizarro was executed for treason and for kidnapping the current Inca, after this short war the Inca signed a legal document joining the real families of the Incas with the real family of Spain in marriage. There is a painting in Lima commemorating this historical moment.





This is how the long-lasting Spanish empire was born

> Aunque viajaron más hombres que mujeres a América, hay ejemplos que indican lo contrario.

### **@clark-z9k**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-DnfGcvZrfA&lc=Ugx-CU59FktAYOYJR7h4AaABAg)

This video, presented by Predictive History, explores the reasons behind the Spanish conquest of the New World, specifically how a small force of Spanish conquistadors managed to subdue millions of indigenous people in the Aztec, Incan, and Mayan civilizations (0:00-0:35). The presenter argues against the purely materialistic view that superior technology and disease were the only factors, proposing instead that the religious beliefs and strict hierarchical structure of the native societies were their ultimate weakness (11:25-12:08).

Here is a detailed breakdown of the discussion:

Context and Global Trade (0:30 - 3:00)
The lecture places the conquest within the context of globalized trade facilitated by the Islamic Golden Age and later the Mongolian Empire (0:35-1:35).
Knowledge transfer included inventions like gunpowder and paper, which later revolutionized European warfare (1:50-2:03).
Exploration: While Vikings were likely the first Europeans to reach the New World, the 16th century marked the violent collision of the old and new worlds (2:08-3:15).

The Wealth of the New World (5:35 - 7:00)
Following the Reconquista of Spain and the rise of the Ottoman Empire blocking trade routes, Spain sought new opportunities (3:20-5:37).
The Pope divided the world between Spain and Portugal, giving Spain the New World, which was rich in gold and silver (5:37-6:09).
Unlike the British who settled the cold, poor regions of North America, the Spanish acquired immense wealth in Central and South America (6:15-6:51).

The Columbian Exchange (6:55 - 8:30)
The collision led to a revolution in European agriculture through the import of nutritious foods like corn, potatoes, peanuts, squash, and tomatoes (7:05-7:58).
This resulted in a massive population explosion in Europe (8:10-8:20).
Conversely, the indigenous population suffered immensely, with roughly 80% wiped out by diseases such as smallpox, measles, typhus, and cholera (8:23-9:37).

The Three Conventional Explanations for Conquest (9:07 - 10:25)
1.  Diseases: Lack of immunity among natives led to demographic collapse (9:17-9:40).
2.  Internal Conflicts: Spanish exploited existing tribal warfare using a divide-and-conquer strategy (9:45-10:10).
3.  Superior Technology: Armor, cannons, and horses provided a military advantage (10:12-10:25).

The Thesis: The Religious Factor (10:30 - 12:08)
The presenter argues that the original Spanish perspective—that their conquest was a divine mission—actually holds logical merit when analyzed through a sociological lens (10:30-11:35).
Main Thesis: The religious practices and beliefs of the Aztecs, Incas, and Mayans made them vulnerable (11:38-11:58).
The Ultimate Weakness: Their societies relied on a strict religious hierarchy where the leader was a divine priest-king. By killing these leaders, the Spanish broke the ultimate taboo, leaving the population hopeless (11:58-12:08).

The Mayan Civilization (13:48 - 18:20)
Known for advanced astronomy, mathematics, and agriculture (14:03-14:30).
Developed the Three Sisters planting technique (corn, beans, squash) to maximize farmland efficiency (15:28-16:15).
Faced ecological collapse around 780 AD due to overpopulation and deforestation (16:48-17:35).

The Aztecs and Human Sacrifice (20:50 - 28:10)
Founded their capital, Tenochtitlan, in swampland, which they adapted into highly productive farmland (21:38-22:30).
The Aztec society was heavily focused on war to capture victims for massive human sacrifice to appease their gods (22:45-23:15).
Hernan Cortez conquered the Aztecs with only a few hundred soldiers, largely because Montezuma treated him as a novel figure rather than an immediate threat (26:00-26:40).
The Spanish utilized smallpox and alliances with rival tribes to defeat the Aztecs (27:55-28:07).

The Inca Civilization (28:12 - 33:10)
Focused on ancestor worship and mummified their divine emperors (28:38-28:55).
Francisco Pizarro captured the Incan emperor Atahualpa (31:17-31:48).
Despite the Incas filling a room with gold as ransom, the Spanish executed Atahualpa, demonstrating that breaking the taboo of killing a divine leader destabilized the empire (32:20-33:05).

Game Theory and the Ultimate Taboo (45:33 - 57:55)
The conquest is analyzed through Game Theory, suggesting that human interactions follow established rules (45:43-46:00).
The native societies established a taboo against attacking city-gods or killing the divine ruler (46:58-47:15).
The Spanish broke these taboos, utilizing psychological warfare to destroy the native worldview, turning them into subjects (50:18-56:45).
Conclusion: Strict hierarchical societies that rely on the worship of a minority by a majority are inherently vulnerable to collapse if the top of that hierarchy is removed (56:50-57:55).

### **@Dioxine-t2s**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-DnfGcvZrfA&lc=UgxMS5uXqceB0APGec14AaABAg)

While this theory of Spanish conquest is good, I'd place it in broader context: superiority (in the sense of superior power) of a more literate civilization against a less literate one. It's just not worldview; it's also knowledge of all sorts: deception, war tactics, administration. Spanish knew you can kill a god because Pizarro surely knew stories of all the conquerors before him who did just that; while Incas were almost completely limited to oral tradition, which is not very good at delivering this sort of endless case studies. Or, in other words, literacy allows cross-polination of minds with the results of endless experiments, while oral tradition, better as it might be at preserving cultural identity, fails at phenomenology. The Spaniards not only appeared to the natives as inhumanly devious and clever, but also proposed much more efficient systems of running society. Incan elites were decimated, but their state apparatus was not completely abolished - it was hijacked and restructured instead.

> Durante tres de los recorridos de visita del obizpo Aguiar y Seijas, en el arzobispado de México hubo 17 matrimonios entre hombres nativos y mujeres no nativas. De estos, 5 fueron con mujeres españolas.  
> Los matrimonios entre mujeres nativas y hombres no nativos durante el mismo periodo fueron 65. De ellos, 17 con hombres españoles.  
> Teniendo en cuenta el relativamente escaso número de españoles que vivían en América, no era una cantidad desdeñable.  
> En el mismo estudio se indica que estos datos corresponden a clases medias, como artesanos, criados, etc.   
>

> Ana de Zaballa Beascoechea.

### **@Eneroscu** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-DnfGcvZrfA&lc=UgwN4u2CRzcdDRcAY754AaABAg)

Professor, you seem to have a major misconception regarding the Spanish conquest of the Americas. To begin with, it did not take place in the 16th century, as you insist, since it began in 1492, which—as everyone knows—belongs to the 15th century.



Furthermore, the idea that Christopher Columbus was of Genoese origin is something that many historians strongly question today, relying on Columbus’s own surviving manuscripts.



And most importantly: Columbus’s voyage was guaranteed with Queen Isabella of Castile’s jewels because no one wanted to finance the expedition. This was due to the fact that Columbus’s calculations about the Earth’s circumference were incorrect. Queen Isabella’s interest and support for the project stemmed from her desire to reach Asia by sailing westward in order to attack the Ottoman Empire from the rear. Her deep Catholic faith shaped her entire reign, and she never showed much interest or expertise in economic matters—quite the opposite of her husband, King Ferdinand of Aragon, who was focused on economic affairs and took no part whatsoever in Columbus’s voyage or in the subsequent conquest of the Americas.

Professor, your understanding of the Spanish conquest of the Americas is extraordinarily confused. You claim that the Pope divided the world into two halves to prevent conflicts between Spain and Portugal, and that this is why Spain “got” the Americas. What you are referring to is the Treaty of Tordesillas, which was signed two years after Columbus arrived in the New World.



You also state that Spain only conquered Central and South America, apparently unaware that Spanish expeditions reached as far north as Alaska.



Then you say that Spain became rich because of the gold and silver found in the Americas, while ignoring the fact that the Crowns of Castile and Aragon were already wealthy within the European context of the time.



Your lack of historical knowledge continues when you argue that, since Spain and Portugal were “in the south” of the Americas, the British had to settle for the north, supposedly the “worst part,” because the natives there were “very aggressive and violent.” When the British and French arrived, Spanish missionaries had already been living peacefully with Indigenous peoples for decades. It was British strategies—such as distributing virus‑infected blankets—and French practices like scalping that led to the large‑scale destruction of native populations.



You also claim that Europeans brought agriculture from the Americas, while the natives received only measles and smallpox. This is simply absurd. Spain brought to the Americas all the knowledge it possessed at the time. Long before the British and French arrived, Spain had already introduced agriculture, livestock, hospitals, and more universities than existed in Europe at that moment. The first Nahuatl (Aztec) dictionary was published in 1555 by Spanish scholars—decades before the English produced their first dictionary in 1604.



I have watched only eight minutes of your video, and I already find it unbearable.



Professor, you demonstrate a profound lack of historical understanding, and you should refrain from misleading your students in this way.

My dude. I love your lectures, but you need to learn a looooooot of Mesoamerican history.
There weren't only Mayans and Aztecs.
Mentioning some of them (and still missing several): civilization started with the Olmecs, then Mayans and Zapotecs, Mixtecs, Cholultecs, Teotihuacans, Toltecs (which by the way built the largest empire in Mesoamerican history), Purepechas (or Tarascans), Huastecs, Totonacs, and all the host of Nahua peoples: Texcocans, Tepanecs, the Xochimilca, Tlaxcalteca and finally (a little less than two hundred years before the Spanish) the Mexica, called Aztecs.

[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-DnfGcvZrfA&lc=UgxU-9_maf-fHJKgDJF4AaABAg)

WOW THIS IS SO WRONG LOL. Firstly, stating that Vikings or Columbus "discovered" the "new" world is a typical dehumanization of Native Americans - So it does not count that they were obviously aware of their own existence? Second, the conquest of Mexico-Tenochtitlan was primarily a civil war in Mesoamerica, conducted 99% by indigenous people from Tlaxcala and other kingdoms. Third, Aztecs don't even exist, they were called Mexica and they were not the dominant group in the entire region as there was none (that's old Spanish and 20th C. Mexican propaganda): The Tarascan empire West of them was stonger for example, and never conquered (like multiple other kingdoms).
p.s. The image in your Mayan calendar slide is neither calendar nor Mayan XD. It's the Mexica sun stone.

ALL THAT SAID, your theory on the local religion being a vulnerability has some validity: Mesoamericans were used to switching gods whenever a kingdom conquered one another and replaced the ruling elite. So when the Spanish (new elite) wanted everyone to become Cristian, the population simply placed the Cristian saints in the altars of the new gods and continued their traditions. The actual transformation into more standard Catholisism would take centuries, in fact the local rituals people practice (outside of churches) are still quite different from the Roman rite.

### **@HectorWPadilla** | 👍 24 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-DnfGcvZrfA&lc=UgxLI0t-Xaxu6V1c-4V4AaABAg)

9:38 “ basically a genocide “

Let’s get it straight—genocide means the intent to destroy a people. The tragic deaths from disease after Spanish contact don’t meet that definition. In contrast, the U.S. government enacted policies against Native Americans that do meet it—forced removals, broken treaties, and cultural erasure.

The Spanish Crown sought to build a colonial society with Indigenous peoples—not eradicate them. Few peninsulares came. Natives built aqueducts, cathedrals, hospitals, and universities. Repúblicas de Indios gave native communities semi-autonomous status, and many Indigenous languages were written and preserved by missionaries. In fact, more native languages were documented under Spanish rule than in modern times.

Spain even held moral debates—like the famous Valladolid Debate—about whether Indigenous peoples had souls. That may sound outrageous today, but it’s still a far cry from Jefferson, who viewed Native Americans as obstacles to be removed.

And let’s be honest—slavery, war, and human sacrifice existed under the Mexicas and Incas before Europeans arrived. These weren’t peaceful, egalitarian societies.

This isn’t about excusing colonization—it’s about questioning the one-dimensional narratives we’ve been handed.

> LOS INDIOS, LA INQUISICIÓN   
> Y LOS TRIBUNALES ECLESIÁSTICOS ORDINARIOS   
> EN NUEVA ESPAÑA. DEF INICIÓN JURISDICCIONAL   
> Y JUSTO PROCESO, 1571-c.1750   
> JORGE E. TRAsLOSHEROS   
> Instituto de Investigaciones Históricas, UNAM   
>   
> "Felipe II mandó fundar el Tribunal del Santo Oficio en Nueva España   
> en el año de 1569. Casi dos años después se instauró en la ciudad de   
> México con la expresa prohibición de que conociera de los crímenes   
> cometidos contra la fe por los indios, asuntos que quedaron bajo la   
> jurisdicción ordinaria de los obispos. No obstante, la relación entre los   
> naturales y la Inquisición estuvo muy lejos de quedar suspendida lo que   
> obligó a una estrecha colaboración entre el Santo Oficio y los tribunales   
> eclesiásticos ordinarios, también llamados audiencias y provisoratos. El   
> ensayo que ahora pongo en manos del lector estudia estas relaciones   
> entre el año de 1571 y mediados del siglo XVIII. Centraremos nuestro   
> esfuerzo en tres asuntos que dan materia, forma y sentido a las acciones  
> de la justicia que estudiamos: primero, revisaremos brevemente   
> la relación entre el rey, los indios y la justicia; segundo, la definición   
> de competencias entre el tribunal del Santo Oficio y los eclesiásticos   
> ordinarios y, tercero, veremos cómo los dos factores anteriores toman   
> cuerpo y se concretan en el esfuerzo por garantizar un proceso justo   
> a los naturales acorde a su personalidad jurídica."

I was reading the nonsense that other Spanish speakers (I assume and hope they are Spanish) commented, and I am horrified by how blind they are. To contextualize the black legend coments: In Latin America and Spain, there is a recent right-wing movement that seeks to reclaim the Spanish legacy and its so-called Golden Age. One of its main arguments is that the Anglo-Saxon world unfairly blamed Spain for the conquest and genocide of Indigenous peoples — portraying it as a process that preceded and contrasted with British colonization. This narrative, according to these interpretations, served to assign moral blame to the Spanish Empire. Consequently, this revisionism presents Spanish colonization as a comprehensive, genuinely civilizing, and morally upright enterprise in all its aspects.

I love your work, especially "Civilization END: The Decline and Fall of the American Empire," and I found your conclusion particularly compelling. However, I believe there was a misuse of the term "genocide." in this video. For an event to be classified as genocide, it must involve the systematic extermination of a group based on political affiliation, ethnicity, religion, or nationality. Referring to the death of 80% of a population due to disease as genocide does not align with this definition, as there was no deliberate intent to exterminate. True examples of genocide would include the Belgian massacres in the Congo, British atrocities against Native Americans, colonial violence in South Africa, New Zealand, and Australia, the Holocaust by Nazi Germany, the U.S. in Vietnam, and Israel’s actions in Palestine. It is essential to use such powerful and historically significant terms with precision.
